Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\n(this is the start of the consultation)\n\n[Last Question]\nHello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 52 / veterinary_technologist_or_technician / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Death_Suicide\nmanifestation: \"What scared me enough to come in is that I keep thinking it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, and sometimes when I’m driving home from the clinic I catch myself wondering what would happen if I didn’t turn the wheel.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]

I’m… I’m a little embarrassed to say it. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still going to work and everything, but what scared me enough to come in is that I keep having these thoughts that it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up.

And sometimes, driving home from the clinic, I’ll catch myself wondering what would happen if I just… didn’t turn the wheel. I know that sounds bad. I don’t want to upset anyone—I just thought I should probably tell someone.

Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.

I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.

I haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.

But after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.

I don’t want to upset anyone—I just thought I should probably tell someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I think. Since this whole intense up-and-down situation started around taking in our relative. I kept thinking it would settle down, so I didn’t want to make too much of it, but… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just collapsed all day, but most days it feels like the color has sort of drained out of everything.\n\nEven when the house is busy and people are around, I feel… empty, I guess. And I’ve been crying in the laundry room sometimes because I don’t want anyone to see me like that. I just feel really hopeless in those moments. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, maybe that’s normal when you’re stressed, but… I haven’t really touched my camera, and that used to be such a big thing for me.\n\nI still notice the bird feeders are there, but I don’t check them with any real interest. And the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs doing, but I just don’t seem to care right now. It’s a little frightening, honestly, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to overstate it. My appetite’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nLately I’ll have coffee, maybe pick at some toast, and then I’ll get through a clinic shift and realize I barely ate anything all day. So… I’m not sure about the exact weight change, but I’m definitely not eating like I normally do. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still get through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t move at all. But everything feels so much heavier than it should.\n\nAt work, just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches can feel like I’m dragging myself through mud. And by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I can barely make dinner or help with the evening care routine. I do it, mostly, but it feels like I’m forcing my body through it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. I’ll walk past the bird feeders and maybe notice they’re there, but I don’t really check them with any interest. And the garden… normally I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs tending, but lately I just don’t seem to care.\n\nThat’s actually kind of frightening to say out loud, because those were the things that made me feel like me. I don’t know if that’s the kind of pleasure you mean.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you felt somewhat better, did you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.\n\nBut after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. Yes… I mean, I know everyone gets frustrated sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.

But I’ve been feeling really guilty about how I react at home. When things feel disrupted or people need something from me, I catch myself resenting it, and then I feel awful for even having that thought. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.

And then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. I know that probably sounds horrible.

It’s a little frightening, honestly, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to overstate it. My appetite’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nLately I’ll have coffee, maybe pick at some toast, and then I’ll get through a clinic shift and realize I barely ate anything all day. So… I’m not sure about the exact weight change, but I’m definitely not eating like I normally do. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still get through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t move at all. But everything feels so much heavier than it should.\n\nAt work, just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches can feel like I’m dragging myself through mud. And by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I can barely make dinner or help with the evening care routine. I do it, mostly, but it feels like I’m forcing my body through it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. I’ll walk past the bird feeders and maybe notice they’re there, but I don’t really check them with any interest. And the garden… normally I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs tending, but lately I just don’t seem to care.\n\nThat’s actually kind of frightening to say out loud, because those were the things that made me feel like me. I don’t know if that’s the kind of pleasure you mean.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you felt somewhat better, did you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.\n\nBut after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know everyone gets frustrated sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nBut I’ve been feeling really guilty about how I react at home. When things feel disrupted or people need something from me, I catch myself resenting it, and then I feel awful for even having that thought. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.\n\nAnd then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. I know that probably sounds horrible.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still get through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t move at all. But everything feels so much heavier than it should.\n\nAt work, just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches can feel like I’m dragging myself through mud. And by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I can barely make dinner or help with the evening care routine. I do it, mostly, but it feels like I’m forcing my body through it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. I’ll walk past the bird feeders and maybe notice they’re there, but I don’t really check them with any interest. And the garden… normally I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs tending, but lately I just don’t seem to care.\n\nThat’s actually kind of frightening to say out loud, because those were the things that made me feel like me. I don’t know if that’s the kind of pleasure you mean.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you felt somewhat better, did you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.\n\nBut after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know everyone gets frustrated sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nBut I’ve been feeling really guilty about how I react at home. When things feel disrupted or people need something from me, I catch myself resenting it, and then I feel awful for even having that thought. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.\n\nAnd then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. I know that probably sounds horrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. A little, yes… though I’m not sure if I’d call it fidgety exactly.

It was more like I couldn’t leave things alone. It’s a little frightening, honestly, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to overstate it. My appetite’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nLately I’ll have coffee, maybe pick at some toast, and then I’ll get through a clinic shift and realize I barely ate anything all day. So… I’m not sure about the exact weight change, but I’m definitely not eating like I normally do. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still get through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t move at all. But everything feels so much heavier than it should.\n\nAt work, just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches can feel like I’m dragging myself through mud. And by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I can barely make dinner or help with the evening care routine. I do it, mostly, but it feels like I’m forcing my body through it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. I’ll walk past the bird feeders and maybe notice they’re there, but I don’t really check them with any interest. And the garden… normally I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs tending, but lately I just don’t seem to care.\n\nThat’s actually kind of frightening to say out loud, because those were the things that made me feel like me. I don’t know if that’s the kind of pleasure you mean.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you felt somewhat better, did you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.\n\nBut after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know everyone gets frustrated sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nBut I’ve been feeling really guilty about how I react at home. When things feel disrupted or people need something from me, I catch myself resenting it, and then I feel awful for even having that thought. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.\n\nAnd then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. I know that probably sounds horrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t heard voices or seen things other people couldn’t see, not that I’m aware of.\n\nSometimes I can get caught up in my own thoughts, but nothing like that. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m not sure if I’d call it fidgety exactly.\n\nIt was more like I couldn’t leave things alone. I took extra shifts, redid the whole household care schedule, started contacting contractors, and was trying to coordinate everyone’s tasks like it was some major hospital project. No one had really asked me to take all that on, but at the time it felt like I was just being helpful and getting things organized.\n\nI guess looking back, it was probably more than was necessary.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t want to upset anyone—I just thought I should probably tell someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I think. Since this whole intense up-and-down situation started around taking in our relative. I kept thinking it would settle down, so I didn’t want to make too much of it, but… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just collapsed all day, but most days it feels like the color has sort of drained out of everything.\n\nEven when the house is busy and people are around, I feel… empty, I guess. And I’ve been crying in the laundry room sometimes because I don’t want anyone to see me like that. I just feel really hopeless in those moments. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, maybe that’s normal when you’re stressed, but… I haven’t really touched my camera, and that used to be such a big thing for me.\n\nI still notice the bird feeders are there, but I don’t check them with any real interest. And the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs doing, but I just don’t seem to care right now. It’s a little frightening, honestly, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to overstate it. My appetite’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nLately I’ll have coffee, maybe pick at some toast, and then I’ll get through a clinic shift and realize I barely ate anything all day. So… I’m not sure about the exact weight change, but I’m definitely not eating like I normally do. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still get through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t move at all. But everything feels so much heavier than it should.\n\nAt work, just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches can feel like I’m dragging myself through mud. And by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I can barely make dinner or help with the evening care routine. I do it, mostly, but it feels like I’m forcing my body through it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. I’ll walk past the bird feeders and maybe notice they’re there, but I don’t really check them with any interest. And the garden… normally I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs tending, but lately I just don’t seem to care.\n\nThat’s actually kind of frightening to say out loud, because those were the things that made me feel like me. I don’t know if that’s the kind of pleasure you mean.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you felt somewhat better, did you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.\n\nBut after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know everyone gets frustrated sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nBut I’ve been feeling really guilty about how I react at home. When things feel disrupted or people need something from me, I catch myself resenting it, and then I feel awful for even having that thought. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.\n\nAnd then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. I know that probably sounds horrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t heard voices or seen things other people couldn’t see, not that I’m aware of.\n\nSometimes I can get caught up in my own thoughts, but nothing like that. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m not sure if I’d call it fidgety exactly.\n\nIt was more like I couldn’t leave things alone. I took extra shifts, redid the whole household care schedule, started contacting contractors, and was trying to coordinate everyone’s tasks like it was some major hospital project. No one had really asked me to take all that on, but at the time it felt like I was just being helpful and getting things organized.\n\nI guess looking back, it was probably more than was necessary.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I was talking a lot more, at least not in a way I noticed.\n\nMaybe I was a little more chatty when I was trying to explain plans or organize things, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. No one specifically told me I was talking too much. Is that what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

And I’ve been crying in the laundry room sometimes because I don’t want anyone to see me like that. I just feel really hopeless in those moments. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, maybe that’s normal when you’re stressed, but… I haven’t really touched my camera, and that used to be such a big thing for me.\n\nI still notice the bird feeders are there, but I don’t check them with any real interest. And the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs doing, but I just don’t seem to care right now. It’s a little frightening, honestly, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to overstate it. My appetite’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nLately I’ll have coffee, maybe pick at some toast, and then I’ll get through a clinic shift and realize I barely ate anything all day. So… I’m not sure about the exact weight change, but I’m definitely not eating like I normally do. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still get through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t move at all. And by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I can barely make dinner or help with the evening care routine. I do it, mostly, but it feels like I’m forcing my body through it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. No one had really asked me to take all that on, but at the time it felt like I was just being helpful and getting things organized.\n\nI guess looking back, it was probably more than was necessary.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I was talking a lot more, at least not in a way I noticed.\n\nMaybe I was a little more chatty when I was trying to explain plans or organize things, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. No one specifically told me I was talking too much. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel more confident in your abilities or more self-important than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling superior or anything like that.\n\nI may have felt a little more capable, like I could handle things if I just stayed organized, but I didn’t think I was unusually important or had special abilities. At least, that’s not how it felt to me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring that time when you felt somewhat better, did you do anything impulsively that you might not normally do?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 52 / veterinary_technologist_or_technician / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Painful_Consequence_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"I bought monitoring equipment, ramps, bedding, and supplies on impulse because it all felt urgently necessary at the time, and only afterward did I realize I’d put charges on the credit card that we really can’t cover.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe… yes. I mean, I didn’t do anything wild exactly, but I did buy a lot of things kind of suddenly.

It felt really necessary at the time — monitoring equipment, ramps, extra bedding, supplies, things like that. I told myself it was responsible, like I was planning ahead and making sure everything was covered. But afterward I realized I’d put it on the credit card, and… we really can’t cover those charges right now.

I feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as impulsive, but it wasn’t really how I’d normally handle it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m… I’m a little embarrassed to say it. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still going to work and everything, but what scared me enough to come in is that I keep having these thoughts that it might be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nAnd sometimes, driving home from the clinic, I’ll catch myself wondering what would happen if I just… didn’t turn the wheel. I know that sounds bad. I don’t want to upset anyone—I just thought I should probably tell someone.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about not waking up or wondering what would happen if you didn't turn the wheel?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about a month, I think. Since this whole intense up-and-down situation started around taking in our relative. I kept thinking it would settle down, so I didn’t want to make too much of it, but… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty most of the time since these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’m still functioning, so I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just collapsed all day, but most days it feels like the color has sort of drained out of everything.\n\nEven when the house is busy and people are around, I feel… empty, I guess. And I’ve been crying in the laundry room sometimes because I don’t want anyone to see me like that. I just feel really hopeless in those moments. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have. I mean, maybe that’s normal when you’re stressed, but… I haven’t really touched my camera, and that used to be such a big thing for me.\n\nI still notice the bird feeders are there, but I don’t check them with any real interest. And the garden… usually I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs doing, but I just don’t seem to care right now. It’s a little frightening, honestly, because those things used to make me feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any change in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: A little, maybe… I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I don’t want to overstate it. My appetite’s been kind of all over the place.\n\nLately I’ll have coffee, maybe pick at some toast, and then I’ll get through a clinic shift and realize I barely ate anything all day. So… I’m not sure about the exact weight change, but I’m definitely not eating like I normally do. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get things done physically?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I still get through the day, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t move at all. But everything feels so much heavier than it should.\n\nAt work, just walking from the treatment area over to the lab benches can feel like I’m dragging myself through mud. And by the time I get home, I’m just… spent. I can barely make dinner or help with the evening care routine. I do it, mostly, but it feels like I’m forcing my body through it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. I’ll walk past the bird feeders and maybe notice they’re there, but I don’t really check them with any interest. And the garden… normally I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs tending, but lately I just don’t seem to care.\n\nThat’s actually kind of frightening to say out loud, because those were the things that made me feel like me. I don’t know if that’s the kind of pleasure you mean.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you felt somewhat better, did you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.\n\nBut after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know everyone gets frustrated sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nBut I’ve been feeling really guilty about how I react at home. When things feel disrupted or people need something from me, I catch myself resenting it, and then I feel awful for even having that thought. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.\n\nAnd then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. I know that probably sounds horrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t heard voices or seen things other people couldn’t see, not that I’m aware of.\n\nSometimes I can get caught up in my own thoughts, but nothing like that. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m not sure if I’d call it fidgety exactly.\n\nIt was more like I couldn’t leave things alone. I took extra shifts, redid the whole household care schedule, started contacting contractors, and was trying to coordinate everyone’s tasks like it was some major hospital project. No one had really asked me to take all that on, but at the time it felt like I was just being helpful and getting things organized.\n\nI guess looking back, it was probably more than was necessary.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I was talking a lot more, at least not in a way I noticed.\n\nMaybe I was a little more chatty when I was trying to explain plans or organize things, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.\n\nAnd then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. I mean, I haven’t heard voices or seen things other people couldn’t see, not that I’m aware of.\n\nSometimes I can get caught up in my own thoughts, but nothing like that. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods where you felt somewhat better, even if only for a few days, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… there was a stretch earlier this month where I felt better, I guess. Maybe too better, if that makes sense.\n\nI was almost… electrically upbeat for a few days, and at first I thought, “Oh good, I’m coming out of it.” But then I was snapping at people over really small things, like a medication cup being misplaced or the morning schedule changing. It wasn’t like how I usually try to handle things.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it didn’t feel entirely like me. Does that count as what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: I still… I mean, I can still appreciate things in theory, I guess. But no, not really in the way I used to.\n\nI haven’t touched my camera, and I used to take pictures all the time just because it made me feel like myself. I’ll walk past the bird feeders and maybe notice they’re there, but I don’t really check them with any interest. And the garden… normally I’d be thinking ahead about what’s coming up, what needs tending, but lately I just don’t seem to care.\n\nThat’s actually kind of frightening to say out loud, because those were the things that made me feel like me. I don’t know if that’s the kind of pleasure you mean.\n\nDoctor: During that stretch when you felt somewhat better, did you have more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I did. I mean, at first I tried to see it as a good thing, like maybe I was finally getting back to normal.\n\nBut after work I couldn’t really settle down. I’d be pacing around the house, measuring rooms for no urgent reason, making lists, reorganizing cabinets… and I’d still be doing it long after everyone else had gone to bed.\n\nIt wasn’t like I was doing anything terrible, exactly. It just felt like I had this motor going and I couldn’t sit still. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I did. I didn’t think of it as a problem at the time, because I felt pretty sharp, honestly.\n\nI was only sleeping maybe three hours a night, but I’d still wake up before sunrise feeling ready to go, like I didn’t really need any more rest. I know that probably sounds a bit off… but at the time it felt almost efficient, like I was just finally functioning better.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel like you could do anything or that you were capable of more than you actually were?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a grand sort of way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI may have felt more confident about getting things done, or like I could finally catch up on everything, but I don’t remember thinking I could do anything impossible or anything like that. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling guilty about anything in particular since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I know everyone gets frustrated sometimes, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic.\n\nBut I’ve been feeling really guilty about how I react at home. When things feel disrupted or people need something from me, I catch myself resenting it, and then I feel awful for even having that thought. I keep telling myself I’m a terrible person for being impatient.\n\nAnd then I replay every look I gave, every sharp comment… like it’s evidence that I’m making everyone’s life harder. Sometimes I think my husband and the family would honestly be better off without me around. I know that probably sounds horrible.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, like hearing voices or seeing things that others can't, since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t heard voices or seen things other people couldn’t see, not that I’m aware of.\n\nSometimes I can get caught up in my own thoughts, but nothing like that. I hope that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety since these symptoms started?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I’m not sure if I’d call it fidgety exactly.\n\nIt was more like I couldn’t leave things alone. I took extra shifts, redid the whole household care schedule, started contacting contractors, and was trying to coordinate everyone’s tasks like it was some major hospital project. No one had really asked me to take all that on, but at the time it felt like I was just being helpful and getting things organized.\n\nI guess looking back, it was probably more than was necessary.\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you find yourself talking more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think I was talking a lot more, at least not in a way I noticed.\n\nMaybe I was a little more chatty when I was trying to explain plans or organize things, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. No one specifically told me I was talking too much. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you feel more confident in your abilities or more self-important than usual?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of feeling superior or anything like that.\n\nI may have felt a little more capable, like I could handle things if I just stayed organized, but I didn’t think I was unusually important or had special abilities. At least, that’s not how it felt to me. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: During that time when you felt somewhat better, did you do anything impulsively that you might not normally do?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes. I mean, I didn’t do anything wild exactly, but I did buy a lot of things kind of suddenly.\n\nIt felt really necessary at the time — monitoring equipment, ramps, extra bedding, supplies, things like that. I told myself it was responsible, like I was planning ahead and making sure everything was covered. But afterward I realized I’d put it on the credit card, and… we really can’t cover those charges right now.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as impulsive, but it wasn’t really how I’d normally handle it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did you feel that somewhat better mood when it happened earlier this month?\n\nPatient: It’s a little hard to separate out, honestly. I wouldn’t say it was just one clear stretch of a few days.\n\nThis whole intense up-and-down crisis around taking in our relative has been going on for about a month now. The “somewhat better” parts were mixed into that, if that makes sense… not like I woke up on Monday and it ended on Friday. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: How many consecutive days did you feel somewhat better at the peak of that improved mood?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t know if I can pin it down to exact consecutive days. At the peak, it still feels like it was part of this whole month-long up-and-down period around taking in our relative.\n\nMaybe there were a few days where I seemed more “okay” or more functional, but I’m not sure I could honestly separate it cleanly. Does that answer it the way you need?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But afterward I realized I’d put it on the credit card, and… we really can’t cover those charges right now.\n\nI feel embarrassed saying that. I don’t know if that counts as impulsive, but it wasn’t really how I’d normally handle it.\n\nDoctor: How many days in a row did you feel that somewhat better mood when it happened earlier this month?\n\nPatient: It’s a little hard to separate out, honestly.
